Boundedness in general type MMP and fivefold effective termination
Jingjun Han, Jihao Liu, Lu Qi, Ziquan Zhuang

Abstract
. We show that in any sequence of a general type MMP, the minimal log discrepancy of singularities takes at most finitely many values, and the fibers of all the extremal contractions and flips belong to a bounded family. As an application, we prove the effective termination of general type MMPs in dimension at most 5 5 . A key ingredient in the proof is an analysis of the behavior of local volumes in the MMP.



Throughout this paper, we work over
\mathbb{C}
, the field of complex numbers.

1.
Introduction

The Minimal Model Program (MMP) aims to construct, for any projective variety, a birational model that is as simple as possible. One of the main open problems in the MMP is the termination of the program. Conjecturally, every sequence of MMP terminates after finitely many steps; in particular, most natural invariants of the variety are expected to be bounded in any given MMP sequence. The purpose of this article is to prove some boundedness statements of this kind, without assuming termination, for varieties of general type, or more generally, for any MMP that starts with a Kawamata log terminal (klt) pair
(X,\Delta)
such that either
K_{X}+\Delta
or
\Delta
is big. As applications, we prove some effective and explicit termination results in dimension at most five.

1.1.
Boundedness

Our first result concerns the boundedness of the index and minimal log discrepancy. Recall that the minimal log discrepancy (mld), denoted
\mathrm{mld}(x,X,\Delta)
, of a klt pair
(X,\Delta)
at a point
x\in X
is the smallest log discrepancy of prime divisors over
x\in X
. It is one of the most important invariants in the study of the MMP, partly due to its monotonicity under the MMP.

Theorem 1.1

(see Theorem
3.1
)
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a projective
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial klt pair such that either
K_{X}+\Delta
or
\Delta
is big. Then there exist a positive integer
r
and a finite set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
, depending only on the pair
(X,\Delta)
, such that for any sequence of steps of a
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP
(X,\Delta)\dashrightarrow(Y,\Delta_{Y})
,

(1)

the Cartier index of any Weil divisor on
Y
is at most
r
, and

(2)

for any (not necessarily closed) point
y\in Y
, we have
\mathrm{mld}(y,Y,\Delta_{Y})\in S
.

Shokurov
[
57
]
has shown that if the minimal log discrepancy of singularities that appear in the MMP satisfies both lower semi-continuity (LSC) and ascending chain condition (ACC), then the MMP must terminate after finitely many steps. Theorem
1.1
confirms the ACC conjecture for minimal log discrepancies in general type MMPs, thus verifies this part of Shokurov’s approach. For some past works on the ACC conjecture in special cases, see for example
[
2
,
5
,
36
,
34
,
28
,
54
,
30
,
37
,
31
]
.

Our second result shows that when running the MMP on varieties of general type (or when the boundary is big), the fibers of all possible extremal contractions and flips belong to a bounded family. This is certainly expected if the MMP terminates. Indeed, one can also show that a log canonical MMP terminates if and only if the pairs that appear in the MMP belong to a bounded family, see Appendix
A.1
.

Theorem 1.2

(see Theorem
4.1
)
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a projective
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial klt pair such that either
K_{X}+\Delta
or
\Delta
is big. Then there exists a projective family
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{B}
over a finite type base
\mathcal{B}
, such that in any sequence of
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP, every fiber of the extremal contractions or the flips is isomorphic to
\mathcal{W}_{b}
for some
b\in\mathcal{B}
.

Recall that a flip
X\dashrightarrow X^{+}
in the MMP is a diagram
X\rightarrow Z\leftarrow X^{+}
where
X\to Z
is the flipping contraction and
X^{+}\to Z
is (also called) the flip
1
1

1

In some literature it is called the flipped contraction.
. We refer to the fiber of
X^{+}\to Z
as the fiber of the flip.

More generally, our method can be used to show that in the setting of Theorem
1.2
, any fixed order infinitesimal neighborhood of the fibers belong to a bounded family. This suggests that there are only finitely many “analytic types” of flips in the MMP, and most invariants of the pair can only change in finitely many different ways in the MMP.

1.2.
Termination in dimension at most five

Termination of the MMP is known in full generality in dimension three by
[
39
,
56
]
. Several special cases are also known in dimension four
[
38
,
22
,
1
,
9
,
58
,
10
,
16
]
, and in higher dimensions we have the termination of MMP with scaling (a special kind of MMP) in the general type or big boundary case
[
8
]
. Beyond these, the termination conjecture remains extremely difficult. In particular, it is an open question whether every fivefold general type MMP terminates. A major application of our main results is the effective termination of general type MMPs, or more generally, of pseudo-effective MMP with a big boundary, in dimension five.

Theorem 1.3

(see Theorem
5.8
)
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a projective klt pair of dimension
5
. Assume that at least one of the following holds.

(1)

K_{X}+\Delta
is big.

(2)

K_{X}+\Delta
is pseudo-effective and
\Delta
is big.

Then there exists a positive integer
m
depending only on
(X,\Delta)
such that every
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP terminates after at most
m
steps.

In dimensions
3
and
4
, we also give some explicit bounds on the number of steps in the MMP. A sample result is the following. Here
h_{4}^{\mathrm{alg}}(X)
is the dimension of the subspace in the singular homology group

H_{4}(X,\mathbb{R})
generated by algebraic cycles.

Theorem 1.4

(see Theorems
6.15
)
.

Let
N
be a positive integer and
(X,\Delta)
a projective log smooth klt pair of dimension
4
such that
N\Delta
has integer coefficients. Assume that

(1)

(X,\Delta)
has at most
N
strata, each stratum has Picard number at most
N
, and
h_{4}^{\mathrm{alg}}(X)\leq N
, and

(2)

\Delta
is big,
\mathrm{vol}(\Delta)\geq\frac{1}{N}
, and
(\Delta\cdot H^{3})\leq N
for some very ample divisor
H
on
X
.

Then any
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP terminates after at most
M^{M}
steps, where
M:=(2N)^{9}!
.

By passing to log resolutions, Theorem
1.4
gives explicit termination results for klt MMP with a big boundary (see Lemma
2.15
).

We remark that even the termination of such fourfold MMPs was not previously known. We also prove similar explicit termination results for general type fourfold MMPs (Theorem
6.14
), terminal fourfold MMPs (Corollary
6.9
) and arbitrary threefold MMPs (Theorem
6.16
). For some past works on the explicit or effective termination problem for threefolds, see for example
[
17
,
53
,
15
,
40
]
.

1.3.
Local volume

A key player in our proof is the local volume of klt singularities, an invariant introduced by Chi Li
[
51
]
with motivation from questions in K-stability. A natural question is how the local volume behaves in the MMP. In general, this invariant does not seem to satisfy any monotonicity in the MMP, see Remark
3.6
. Nonetheless, one of our observations is that the local volumes are bounded away from zero in the MMP, at least when the boundary is big. By standard argument (as in
[
8
]
), any general type MMP can be turned into an MMP with a big boundary.

Theorem 1.5

(see Theorem
3.2
)
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a projective
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial klt pair such that
\Delta
is big. Then there exists some
\varepsilon>0
such that for any sequence of steps of a
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP
(X,\Delta)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
and any closed point
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
, we have
\widehat{\rm vol}(x^{\prime},X^{\prime})\geq\varepsilon
.

Here
\widehat{\rm vol}(x^{\prime},X^{\prime})
denotes the local volume of the klt singularity
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
(see Section
2.3
). To prove this theorem, we further introduce the
log canonical volume
(see Section
3.2
) of the big
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
\Delta
, defined as the largest volume of a graded linear series
V_{\bullet}
of
\Delta
such that
\left(X,\frac{1}{m}\Gamma\right)
is log canonical for any positive integer
m
and any
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
\Gamma
in the linear system
|V_{m}|
. It turns out that the log canonical volume is always non-decreasing in the MMP (Lemma
3.11
), and the local volume of singularities is always bounded from below by the log canonical volume (Lemma
3.12
), which implies Theorem
1.5
. On the other hand, the local volume controls many other invariants of the singularity. In particular, by the local K-stability theory of klt singularities (see
[
69
]
for a recent survey), the index and minimal log discrepancy of a klt singularity are bounded from above once the local volume is bounded away from zero, and Theorem
1.1
follows from this.

Local volume also plays an important role in the proof of Theorem
1.2
. A basic idea is that if
(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})\to Z
is an extremal contraction in a
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP and
\Delta
is big, then the local volume of the relative cone (see Section
4.2
for the construction) of
(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
over
Z
is also bounded from below by the log canonical volume of
\Delta
(up to a suitable multiplicative constant that only depends on the coefficients of
\Delta
), see Theorem
4.10
. The local K-stability theory then implies that the relative cones are at least specially bounded (namely, they are bounded up to degenerations induced by special test configurations), though in this case a significant amount of work is still needed to deduce the boundedness in Theorem
1.2
from the above special boundedness.

1.4.
Outline of the article

In Section
2
we summarize some preliminary results that will be used in the later part of this paper. In Section
3
we prove Theorems
1.1
and
1.5
. In Section
4
we prove Theorem
1.2
. In Section
5
we prove Theorem
1.3
. In Section
6
we prove Theorem
1.4
and its variants. In the appendix, we prove that if the pairs that appear in a log canonical MMP are bounded, then the MMP terminates after finitely many steps.

2.
Preliminary

In this section, we collect some preliminary materials that will be used throughout the article. We suggest skimming through most of this section on first reading and returning to the relevant subsections for more details when certain definitions and statements are needed.

2.1.
Notation and conventions

In this paper, varieties are assumed to be quasi-projective unless otherwise specified. We follow the standard terminology from
[
42
,
47
]
.

Given two
\mathbb{R}
-divisors
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
on a variety
X
, we write
\Delta^{\prime}\geq\Delta
if
\mathrm{mult}_{D}\Delta^{\prime}\geq\mathrm{mult}_{D}\Delta
for any prime divisor
D
on
X
.

A
sub-pair

(X,\Delta)
consists of a normal variety
X
together with an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
\Delta
on
X
(a priori, we do not require that
K_{X}+\Delta
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier). It is called a
pair
if
\Delta\geq 0
. We denote by
\mathrm{Coeff}(\Delta)
the (finite) set of coefficients of
\Delta
(in particular, we require that
0\in\mathrm{Coeff}(\Delta)
). A
log (sub-)pair
is a (sub-)pair
(X,\Delta)
such that
K_{X}+\Delta
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier. A
singularity

x\in(X,\Delta)
consists of a pair
(X,\Delta)
and a closed point
x\in X
. If there is no confusion, we shall assume that
X
is affine and
x
is contained in any irreducible component of
\mathrm{Supp}(\Delta)
.

A sub-pair
(X,\Delta)
is called
log smooth
if
X
is smooth and
\mathrm{Supp}(\Delta)
is a simple normal crossing divisor. A
stratum
(resp.
open stratum
) of a log smooth pair
(X,\Delta=\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}B_{i})
, where
B_{i}
are the irreducible components of
\Delta
, is defined to be a connected component of
B_{J}:=\cap_{j\in J}B_{j}
(resp.
B^{\circ}_{J}:=\cap_{j\in J}B_{j}\setminus\cup_{j\not\in J}B_{j}
) for some
J\subseteq I
(by convention,
B_{\emptyset}=X
and
B^{\circ}_{\emptyset}=X\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\Delta)
).

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a log sub-pair. A proper birational morphism
\pi\colon(Y,\Delta_{Y})\rightarrow(X,\Delta)
from a sub-pair is called
crepant
if
K_{Y}+\Delta_{Y}=\pi^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta)
, in which case we also call
(Y,\Delta_{Y})
the
crepant pullback
of
(X,\Delta)
(via
\pi
).

An
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D
on a variety
X
is
big
if
D=A+E
for some ample
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
A
and some
E\geq 0
. Let
X\to Z
be a morphism. We say
D
is
big over
Z
if
D\sim_{\mathbb{R},Z}D^{\prime}
for some big
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D^{\prime}
on
X
.

Let
f\colon X\to Z
be a morphism and
z\in Z
a point. We denote by
X_{z}:=f^{-1}(z)
the (scheme-theoretic) fiber over
z
.

An
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D
on a projective variety
X
is
pseudo-effective
if its numerical class is a limit of
\mathbb{R}
-divisors
D_{i}\geq 0
(
i=1,2,\dots
). Given a projective morphism
X\to Z
, we say
D
is
pseudo-effective over
Z
if its restriction to the fiber
X_{\eta}
is pseudo-effective, where
\eta\in Z
is the image of the generic point of
X
.

A
test configuration
of a pair
(X,\Delta)
consists of the following data:

(1)

A pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})
and a flat morphism
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

(2)

A
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})
lifting the canonical multiplication action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

(3)

An isomorphism
(\mathcal{X}_{t},\mathcal{D}_{t})\cong(X,\Delta)
for all
0\neq t\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

A
fibration
is a proper surjective morphism
f\colon X\to Z
of normal varieties such that
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Z}
. A
fibration germ
, denoted by
f\colon X\to Z\ni z
, consists of a fibration
f\colon X\to Z
where
Z
is affine and a closed point
z\in Z
. If
X\to Z
is a fibration and
(X,\Delta)
is a pair, by abuse of notation we also call
(X,\Delta)\to Z
a fibration.

Let
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
be a birational map and let
D
be an
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
Y
. The
birational pullback

f^{*}D
is defined as
p_{*}q^{*}D
where
p:Z\to X
,
q:Z\to Y
is a resolution of the indeterminacy of
f
(one can check that the definition is independent of the resolution).

Let
X
be a variety. For any proper birational morphism
\pi\colon Y\to X
and any prime divisor
E
on
Y
, we call
E
a
(prime) divisor over

X
and denote by
\mathrm{Center}_{X}(E):=\pi(E)
the
center
of
E
on
X
. We also denote by
c_{X}(E)
the generic point of
\mathrm{Center}_{X}(E)
and sometimes also call it the center of
E
if no confusion arises. We also call
E
as a
(prime) divisor over

x\in X
where
x
is the generic point of
\pi(E)
. We denote by
\mathrm{Ex}(\pi)\subseteq Y
the exceptional locus of
\pi
. The
discrepancy
of
E
with respect to a log sub-pair
(X,\Delta)
, denoted by
a(E,X,\Delta)
, is defined by

a(E,X,\Delta):=\mathrm{mult}_{E}(K_{Y}-\pi^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta)).

The
log discrepancy
of
E
with respect to
(X,\Delta)
is defined by
A_{X,\Delta}(E):=1+a(E,X,\Delta)
.

The notation of
terminal
,
klt
,
plt
,
dlt
, and
log canonical
(sub-)pairs are defined as in
[
42
, Definitions 2.34 and 2.37]
. The
non-klt locus
of a log pair
(X,\Delta)
is defined as

\mathrm{Nklt}(X,\Delta):=\{x\in X\,|\,(X,\Delta)\mbox{ is not klt near }x\}.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a log canonical pair. A subvariety
W\subseteq X
is called a
log canonical center
if either
W=X
, or
W
is the center of some divisor
E
over
X
with
A_{X,\Delta}(E)=0
.

2.2.
Log discrepancy

In this and the next two subsections, we recall some invariants of pairs and their basic properties.

Definition 2.1
.

For any log canonical sub-pair
(X,\Delta)
and a (not necessarily closed) point
x\in X
, we define

\mathrm{mld}(x,X,\Delta):=\inf\{A_{X,\Delta}(E)\mid E\text{ is a prime divisor over }x\in X\}

as the
minimal log discrepancy
(
mld
) of
(X,\Delta)
at
x
, and define

\mathrm{mld}(X,\Delta):=\inf\{A_{X,\Delta}(E)\mid E\text{ is a prime divisor over }X\}

as the
total minimal log discrepancy
(
total mld
) of
(X,\Delta)
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
n
and
r
be two positive integers and
S_{0}\subseteq[0,1]
a finite set. Then there exists a discrete set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
depending only on
n,r
and
S_{0}
satisfying the following. Assume that

(1)

(X,\Delta)
is a dlt pair of dimension
n
,

(2)

\mathrm{Coeff}(\Delta)\subseteq S_{0}
,

(3)

rL
is Cartier for any
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier Weil divisor
L
on
X
, and

(4)

W
is a log canonical center of
(X,\Delta)
of dimension
\geq 1
and
(W,\Delta_{W})
is the dlt pair induced by adjunction

K_{W}+\Delta_{W}:=(K_{X}+\Delta)|_{W}.

Then for any prime divisor
E
over
W
, we have
A_{W,\Delta_{W}}(E)\in S
.

Proof.

By
[
30
]
*Theorem 5.6, there exist real numbers
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in(0,1]
and a positive integer
N
depending only on
n
and
S_{0}
, and
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors
\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{k}\geq 0
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}=1
,
\Delta=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}\Delta_{i}
, each pair
(X,\Delta_{i})
is dlt, and
N\Delta_{i}
is a Weil divisor for each
i
. In particular,
W
is a log canonical center of each
(X,\Delta_{i})
. Let
K_{W}+\Delta_{W,i}:=(K_{X}+\Delta_{i})|_{W}
for each
i
, then each
(W,\Delta_{W,i})
is dlt and
\Delta_{W}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}\Delta_{W,i}
. For each
i
, since
Nr(K_{X}+\Delta_{i})
is Cartier by assumption (3), we have
Nr(K_{W}+\Delta_{W,i})
is also Cartier. Thus the set

S=\frac{1}{Nr}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}a_{i}\middle|\,m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

satisfies the statement of the lemma.
∎

2.3.
Local volume

Let
X
be a variety and
x\in X
a closed point. A
valuation
on the function field
\mathbb{C}(X)
of a variety
X
, also called a
valuation on
X
, is an
\mathbb{R}
-valued function
v:\mathbb{C}(X)^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
v|_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}=0
,
v(ab)=v(a)+v(b)
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{C}(X)^{*}
, and
v(a+b)\geq\min\{v(a),v(b)\}
if
a+b\neq 0
. We say that
v
is a valuation over
x\in X
if
v
is centered at
x
. The set of valuations on
X
is denoted by
\mathrm{Val}_{X}
. The set of valuations over
x\in X
is denoted by
\mathrm{Val}_{X,x}
.

Given a klt sub-pair
(X,\Delta)
, the log discrepancy function

A_{X,\Delta}\colon\mathrm{Val}_{X}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\},

is defined as in
[
35
]
and
[
14
]
*Theorem 3.1. For a divisorial valuation
v=\lambda\cdot\mathrm{ord}_{E}
(where
\lambda>0
and
E
is a prime divisor over
X
), one has
A_{X,\Delta}(v)=\lambda\cdot A_{X,\Delta}(E)
. Since
(X,\Delta)
is klt,
A_{X,\Delta}(v)>0
for all
v\in\mathrm{Val}_{X,x}
. However, it is possible that
A_{X,\Delta}(v)=+\infty
for some
v\in\mathrm{Val}_{X}
, see e.g.
[
35
]
*Remark 5.12.

The volume of a valuation
v\in\mathrm{Val}_{X,x}
is defined as

\mathrm{vol}(v)=\mathrm{vol}_{X,x}(v)=\limsup_{m\to+\infty}\frac{\ell(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak{a}_{m}(v))}{m^{n}/n!},

where
n=\dim X
and
\mathfrak{a}_{m}(v)
denotes the valuation ideal, i.e.

\mathfrak{a}_{m}(v):=\{f\in\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\mid v(f)\geq m\}.

Thanks to the works of
[
20
,
49
,
18
]
, the above limsup is actually a limit.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a klt pair of dimensional
n
and
x\in X
a closed point. For any
v\in\mathrm{Val}_{X,x}
, we define the
normalized volume
of
v
as

\widehat{\rm vol}_{X,\Delta}(v):=\begin{cases}A_{X,\Delta}(v)^{n}\cdot\mathrm{vol}_{X,x}(v),&\mbox{if }A_{X,\Delta}(v)<+\infty,\\
+\infty,&\mbox{if }A_{X,\Delta}(v)=+\infty.\end{cases}

The
local volume
of
(X,\Delta)
at
x
is defined as

\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta):=\inf_{v\in\mathrm{Val}_{X,x}}\widehat{\rm vol}_{X,\Delta}(v).

By
[
51
]
*Theorem 1.2, the local volume of a klt singularity is always positive. By
[
13
]
, there always exists some valuation
v
such that
\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)=\widehat{\rm vol}_{X,\Delta}(v)
. We also need the following properties of the local volume.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
64
]
*Corollary 1.4)
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a klt pair of dimensional
n
, let
x\in X
be a closed point, and let
D
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier Weil divisor on
X
. Then
rD
is Cartier near
x
for some positive integer
r\leq\frac{n^{n}}{\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)}
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists some constant
A>0
depending only on
n
and
\varepsilon
, such that for any
n
-dimensional klt pair
(X,\Delta)
satisfying
\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)\geq\varepsilon
for all closed points
x\in X
, we have

\mathrm{mld}(x,X,\Delta)\leq A

for all (not necessarily) closed points
x\in X
.

Proof.

By
[
4
]
*Proposition 2.3, it suffices to prove that
\mathrm{mld}(x,X,\Delta)\leq A
for all closed point
x\in X
. We are done by
[
65
, Theorem 1.3]
or
[
29
]
*Corollary 2.7.
∎

2.4.
Log canonical threshold

For any log canonical sub-pair
(X,\Delta)
and any
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D
on
X
, we denote by

\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D):=\sup\{\,t\,|\,(X,\Delta+tD)\text{ is log canonical}\,\}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}

the
log canonical threshold
of
D
with respect to
(X,\Delta)
. For any
x\in X
, we also denote by

\mathrm{lct}_{x}(X,\Delta;D):=\sup\{\,t\,|\,(X,\Delta+tD)\text{ is log canonical at }x\,\}

the log canonical threshold at
x
. The following result shows how the log canonical thresholds behave when we perturb the boundary.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a klt pair and let
D,D^{\prime}\geq 0
be
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisors on
X
. Then

\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D+D^{\prime})\geq\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D)}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D^{\prime})}\right)^{-1}.

If
(X,\Delta+D^{\prime})
is log canonical, then we also have

\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta+D^{\prime};D)\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D^{\prime})}\right)\cdot\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D).

Proof.

Let
a:=\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D)
and
a^{\prime}:=\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D^{\prime})
. Then
(X,\Delta+aD)
and
(X,\Delta+a^{\prime}D^{\prime})
are log canonical, so the convex combination

\left(X,\Delta+\frac{a^{\prime}}{a+a^{\prime}}\cdot aD+\frac{a}{a+a^{\prime}}\cdot a^{\prime}D^{\prime}\right)=\left(X,\Delta+\frac{aa^{\prime}}{a+a^{\prime}}(D+D^{\prime})\right)

is log canonical, which gives the first inequality
\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D+D^{\prime})\geq\frac{aa^{\prime}}{a+a^{\prime}}=\left(\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{a^{\prime}}\right)^{-1}
. If
a^{\prime}\geq 1
, then the convex combination

\left(X,\Delta+\frac{a^{\prime}-1}{a^{\prime}}\cdot aD+\frac{1}{a^{\prime}}\cdot a^{\prime}D^{\prime}\right)=\left(X,\Delta+D^{\prime}+\frac{a^{\prime}-1}{a^{\prime}}\cdot aD\right)

is log canonical, which implies the second inequality
\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta+D^{\prime};D)\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{a^{\prime}}\right)a
.
∎

The next statement is a version of Izumi’s inequality and should be well-known to experts. It will be useful in proving explicit termination results in Section
6
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, let
(X,\Delta)
be a log smooth sub-pair such that the coefficients of
\Delta
are at most
1-\varepsilon
, and let
D\geq 0
be an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
X
. Then for any closed point
x\in\mathrm{Supp}(D)
, we have

\mathrm{lct}_{x}(X,\Delta;D)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathrm{mult}_{x}D}.

Proof.

By removing the components of
\Delta
with negative coefficients, we obtain an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
\Delta^{\geq 0}
with
\mathrm{lct}_{x}(X,\Delta;D)\geq\mathrm{lct}_{x}(X,\Delta^{\geq 0};D)
. Possibly replacing
\Delta
with
\Delta^{\geq 0}
, it suffices to prove the lemma when
\Delta\geq 0
. Possibly replacing
D
with a multiple, we may also assume that
\mathrm{mult}_{x}D=1
. We need to show that
x\in(X,\Delta+\varepsilon D)
is log canonical.

Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\to X
be the blowup of
x
and let
E
be the exceptional divisor. Let
\widetilde{\Delta}
(resp.
\widetilde{D}
) be the strict transform of
\Delta
(resp.
D
) on
\tilde{X}
. Since
\mathrm{mult}_{x}D=1
, we have
\widetilde{D}|_{E}\sim_{\mathbb{R}}H
where
H
is the hyperplane class on
E
under the natural isomorphism
E\cong\mathbb{P}^{\dim X-1}
. Note that
(E,\widetilde{\Delta}|_{E})
is a toric pair and the coefficients of
\widetilde{\Delta}|_{E}
are at most
1-\varepsilon
, thus
\mathrm{lct}(E,\widetilde{\Delta}|_{E};G)\geq\varepsilon
for any torus invariant
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
0\leq G\sim_{\mathbb{R}}H
on
E
. By
[
12
]
*Theorem F, we deduce that
\mathrm{lct}(E,\widetilde{\Delta}|_{E};G)\geq\varepsilon
for any
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
0\leq G\sim_{\mathbb{R}}H
on
E
. In particular, the pair
(E,(\widetilde{\Delta}+\varepsilon\widetilde{D})|_{E})
is log canonical. By inversion of adjunction (cf.
[
42
]
*Theorem 5.50), the pair
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\Delta}+\varepsilon\widetilde{D}+E)
is log canonical near
E
. Since the coefficients of
\Delta
are at most
1-\varepsilon
, we have
A_{X,\Delta}(E)\geq\varepsilon
, hence
A_{X,\Delta+\varepsilon D}(E)=A_{X,\Delta}(E)-\varepsilon\cdot\mathrm{mult}_{x}D\geq 0
. Then
K_{\widetilde{X}}+\widetilde{\Delta}+\varepsilon\widetilde{D}+E\geq\pi^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta+\varepsilon D)
, so
(X,\Delta+\varepsilon D)
is log canonical at
x
.
∎

2.5.
Linear series

For any
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D
on a normal variety
X
, we set

H^{0}(X,D):=\{0\neq s\in\mathbb{C}(X)\,|\,\mathrm{div}(s)+D\geq 0\}\cup\{0\}.

Any finite dimensional subspace
V
of
H^{0}(X,D)
is called a
linear series
of
D
. It defines a
linear system

|V|:=\{D^{\prime}=\mathrm{div}(s)+D\,|\,0\neq s\in V\}\cong\mathbb{P}(V).

A graded linear series
V_{\bullet}
of
D
is a sequence of finite dimensional subspaces
V_{m}\subseteq H^{0}(X,mD)
(one for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
) such that
V_{m}\cdot V_{l}\subseteq V_{m+l}
for all
m,l\in\mathbb{N}
. Its volume is defined as

(2.1)

\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet}):=\limsup_{m\to+\infty}\frac{\dim(V_{m})}{m^{n}/n!}

where
n=\dim X
. We denote by
M(V_{\bullet})\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{+}
the semigroup of those positive integers
m
with
V_{m}\neq 0
. If
D
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier, and
\Delta\geq 0
is an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor such that
(X,\Delta)
is klt, we also define the
alpha invariant
of
V_{\bullet}
as

\alpha(X,\Delta;V_{\bullet}):=\inf\left\{\,\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;\Gamma)\,\middle|\ m\in M(V_{\bullet}),\,\Gamma\in\frac{1}{m}|V_{m}|\,\right\}

and simply write
\alpha(V_{\bullet})
if the klt pair
(X,\Delta)
is clear from the context. When
V_{\bullet}
is the complete linear series of
D
, the corresponding alpha invariant is denoted by
\alpha(X,\Delta;D)
.

We say that a graded linear series
V_{\bullet}
is
eventually birational
, if the rational map
X\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}(V^{\vee}_{m})
induced by the linear system
|V_{m}|
is birational onto its image for all sufficiently large
m\in M(V_{\bullet})
. This condition is preserved under birational contractions; by the following lemma, it also ensures that the limsup in (
2.1
) is a limit. For this reason, in the rest of this paper we will exclusively consider graded linear series that are eventually birational.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a klt pair and let
V_{\bullet}
be an eventually birational graded linear series such that
\alpha(V_{\bullet})>0
. Then
\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet})>0
and we have

(2.2)

\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet})=\lim_{M(V_{\bullet})\ni m\to+\infty}\frac{\dim(V_{m})}{m^{n}/n!}.

Proof.

We refer to
[
49
]
for the construction of Okounkov bodies and their general properties. It is clear that
\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet})>0
as
|V_{m}|
defines a birational map for large enough
m
. To prove (
2.2
), let
\pi\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be the blowup of some smooth point
x\in X
and consider the Okounkov body
\Delta(V_{\bullet})
constructed using a full flag of subvarieties of the form
X^{\prime}\supseteq E\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\{\mathrm{pt\}}
, where
E
is the exceptional divisor of the blowup. Since
\alpha(V_{\bullet})>0
, there exists some constant
C>0
such that
\mathrm{mult}_{E}(\pi^{*}\Gamma)=\mathrm{mult}_{x}(\Gamma)\leq Cm
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Gamma\in|V_{m}|
. As
E\cong\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is projective, by
[
49
]
*Lemma 1.11 we know that
\Delta(V_{\bullet})
satisfies condition (A) from
[
49
]
*Definition 2.4. Since
V_{\bullet}
is eventually birational, it also satisfies condition (B) from
[
49
]
*Definition 2.5. By
[
49
]
*Theorem 2.13, we see that

\lim_{M(V_{\bullet})\ni m\to+\infty}\frac{\dim(V_{m})}{m^{n}/n!}

exists and equals
\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet})
.
∎

We also need the following Bertini type result.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a klt pair and let
H
be an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor on
X
. Then there exist some
\delta>0
and some
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any positive integer
m\geq m_{0}
, there exists some
D\in|mH|
with

\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D)\geq\delta.

Proof.

Let
m_{0}
be a sufficiently large and divisible integer such that
|m_{0}H|
is base point free and
|mH|\neq\emptyset
for all
m\geq m_{0}
. For each
i=0,1,\dots,m_{0}-1
, choose some
D_{i}\in|(m_{0}+i)H|
and let

\delta:=\min_{0\leq i\leq m_{0}-1}\{\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D_{i})\}>0.

For any integer
m\geq m_{0}
we may write
m=(\ell+1)m_{0}+i
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and some
0\leq i\leq m_{0}-1
. By our choice of
m_{0}
and
\delta
, we know that
|\ell m_{0}H|
is base point free and
(X,\Delta+\delta D_{i})
is log canonical, thus by Bertini’s theorem, there exists some
D^{\prime}\in|\ell m_{0}H|
such that
(X,\Delta+\delta D_{i}+D^{\prime})
is still log canonical. Note that
\delta\leq 1
. Then
D=D_{i}+D^{\prime}\in|mH|
satisfies
\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D)\geq\delta
.
∎

2.6.
Minimal Model Program (MMP)

We refer to e.g.
[
42
, Paragraph 3.31]
for a description of the MMP. In this subsection, we recall some results on the MMP that will be used in this paper. First we recall the two basic operations in the MMP.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial log canonical pair and let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Z
be a fibration. Then
f
is called a
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-
flipping contraction
if

(1)

f
is small birational,

(2)

the relative Picard number
\rho(X/Z)=1
, and

(3)

-(K_{X}+\Delta)
is
f
-ample.

The
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-
flip

f^{+}\colon X^{+}\longrightarrow Z
of the flipping contraction
f\colon X\longrightarrow Z
is a small birational fibration
f^{+}\colon X^{+}\longrightarrow Z
such that
K_{X^{+}}+\Delta^{+}
is
f^{+}
-ample, where
\Delta^{+}
is the strict transform of
\Delta
on
X^{+}
. When the pair
(X,\Delta)
is clear from the context, we shall refer to
f
(resp.
f^{+}
) as simply the flipping contraction (resp. the flip). By abuse of terminology, we also call the composition
(f^{+})^{-1}\circ f\colon X\dashrightarrow X^{+}
a flip (however, the fibers of a flip only refer to the fibers of
f^{+}
).

Definition 2.11
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial log canonical pair and let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Z
be a projective morphism of normal varieties. Then
f
is called a
divisorial contraction
if

(1)

f
is birational with exceptional locus a divisor,

(2)

the relative Picard number
\rho(X/Z)=1
, and

(3)

-(K_{X}+\Delta)
is
f
-ample.

For some of our proofs, it will be convenient to work with a broader class of birational maps as follows.

Definition 2.12
.

A birational map
\varphi\colon(X,\Delta)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
of log pairs is called an
MMP type contraction
if
\varphi^{-1}
has no exceptional divisor, and there exist proper birational morphisms
f\colon Y\to X
,
g\colon Y\to X^{\prime}
such that

f^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta)-g^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+\Delta^{\prime})\geq 0

and
g=\varphi\circ f
. In particular, we have
\varphi_{*}\Delta\geq\Delta^{\prime}
.

Observe that if
\varphi\colon(X,\Delta)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
is an MMP type contraction and
(X,\Delta)
is klt (resp. log canonical), then so is
(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
. As the name suggests, if
\varphi\colon(X,\Delta)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
is obtained by running a
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP, then
\varphi\colon(X,\Delta)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
is an MMP type contraction. If
\Delta^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}\Delta
, then the birational contraction

\varphi\colon(X,\Delta)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
is an MMP type contraction if and only if
\varphi
is
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-non-positive in the sense of
[
8
]
*Definition 3.6.1. We will also frequently use the following simple observation.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\varphi\colon(X,\Delta+D)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}+D^{\prime})
be an MMP type contraction with
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime},D\geq 0
and
D^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}D
. Let
\Gamma\geq 0
be an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
X
such that
K_{X}+\Gamma\sim_{\mathbb{R}}\mu(K_{X}+D)
for some
\mu\geq 0
, and let
\Gamma^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}\Gamma
. Then
\varphi
also induces an MMP type contraction
\varphi:(X,\mu\Delta+\Gamma)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\mu\Delta^{\prime}+\Gamma^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
f:Y\rightarrow X,g:Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}
be two proper birational morphisms such that

E:=f^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta+D)-g^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+\Delta^{\prime}+D^{\prime})\geq 0

and
g=\varphi\circ f
. So

(2.3)

\mu E=f^{*}(\mu K_{X}+\mu\Delta+\mu D)-g^{*}(\mu K_{X^{\prime}}+\mu\Delta^{\prime}+\mu D^{\prime})\geq 0.

Since
K_{X}+\Gamma\sim_{\mathbb{R}}\mu(K_{X}+D)
and
\varphi^{-1}
does not contract any divisor,

K_{X^{\prime}}+\Gamma^{\prime}-\mu(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})=g_{*}f^{*}(K_{X}+\Gamma-\mu(K_{X}+D))\sim_{\mathbb{R}}0.

Thus by the negativity lemma, we have

(2.4)

f^{*}(K_{X}+\Gamma-\mu(K_{X}+D))-g^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+\Gamma^{\prime}-\mu(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime}))=0,

as the left hand side is
g
-exceptional and
\sim_{\mathbb{R}}0
.
Combining (
2.3
) and (
2.4
), we get

\mu E=f^{*}(K_{X}+\mu\Delta+\Gamma)-g^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+\mu\Delta^{\prime}+\Gamma^{\prime})\geq 0.

Hence
\varphi:(X,\mu\Delta+\Gamma)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\mu\Delta^{\prime}+\Gamma^{\prime})
is an MMP type contraction.
∎

We also introduce a more flexible version of the MMP, where each step of the MMP is a contraction of an extremal face rather than an extremal ray. To avoid confusion, we call such MMP as “face-contracting MMP”. Face-contracting MMP naturally appears in the proofs of the special termination (
cf
.
[
23
]
). The work
[
48
]
also uses special face-contracting MMP in order to ensure that the scaling numbers strictly drop. We remark that projectivity and
\mathbb{Q}
-factoriality of the ambient variety are not automatically presumed in the face-contracting MMP.

Definition 2.14

(Face-contracting MMP)
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a log pair. A
(birational) face-contracting

(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP is a sequence of birational contractions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,\Delta)=:(X_{0},\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1},\Delta_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{i},\Delta_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}

where
(X_{i},\Delta_{i})
is a log pair and
\Delta_{i+1}:=(\varphi_{i})_{*}\Delta_{i}
for each
i
, and each
\varphi_{i}
fits into a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}^{+}}

satisfying the following:

(1)

f_{i}
and
f_{i}^{+}
are birational fibrations,

(2)

f_{i}^{+}
is small,

(3)

-(K_{X_{i}}+\Delta_{i})
is
f_{i}
-ample, and

(4)

K_{X_{i+1}}+\Delta_{i+1}
is
f_{i}^{+}
-ample.

We show that every step of a face-contracting MMP is crepant equivalent to a sequence of divisorial contractions and flips.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a log canonical pair and let
\pi\colon(Y,D)\to(X,\Delta)
be a birational morphism from a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial dlt pair such that
K_{Y}+D=\pi^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta)+E
for some
\pi
-exceptional
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
E\geq 0
. Then any face-contracting
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,\Delta)=:(X_{0},\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1},\Delta_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{i},\Delta_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}

can be lifted to a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,D)=:(Y_{0},D_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{1},D_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{i},D_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,\Delta)=:(X_{0},\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1},\Delta_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{i},\Delta_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}=\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}

where each
(Y_{i},D_{i})
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial dlt pair and each
\psi_{i}
is a sequence of
(K_{Y_{i}}+D_{i})
-MMP. Moreover, for all
i\geq 1
we have
K_{Y_{i}}+D_{i}=\pi_{i}^{*}(K_{X_{i}}+\Delta_{i})
.

Proof.

Let
f_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow Z_{i}
and
f_{i}^{+}:X_{i+1}\rightarrow Z_{i}
be the birational fibrations associated to
\varphi_{i}
. We prove the lemma by induction on
i
. When
i=0
, we simply set
(Y_{0},D_{0}):=(Y,D)
and
\pi_{0}:=\pi
. For
i\geq 1
, suppose that
(Y_{i-1},D_{i-1})
has been constructed. By
[
42
]
*Lemma 3.38 and
[
8
]
*Lemma 3.6.6(4),
(X_{i},\Delta_{i})
is the ample model over
Z_{i-1}
of
(Y_{i-1},D_{i-1})
. In particular,
(Y_{i-1},D_{i-1})
has a minimal model over
Z_{i-1}
. Since the pair
(Y_{i-1},D_{i-1})
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial dlt by induction hypothesis, by
[
11
]
*Corollary 1.2 and Theorem 1.9 or
[
26
, Corollaries 1.8 and 2.9]
, we may run a
(K_{Y_{i-1}}+D_{i-1})
-MMP over
Z_{i-1}
with scaling of an ample divisor, and the MMP terminates with a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial dlt minimal model over
Z_{i-1}
. This gives
\psi_{i-1}:(Y_{i-1},D_{i-1})\dashrightarrow(Y_{i},D_{i})
, where
K_{Y_{i}}+D_{i}
is nef over
Z_{i-1}
. Then
(X_{i},\Delta_{i})
is the ample model over
Z_{i-1}
of
(Y_{i},D_{i})
, so there exists an induced birational morphism
\pi_{i}:Y_{i}\to X_{i}
such that
K_{Y_{i}}+D_{i}=\pi_{i}^{*}(K_{X_{i}}+\Delta_{i})
.
This finishes the induction.
∎

We can also lift an MMP to the terminalization at the price of possibly changing the terminal boundary during the process.

Definition 2.16
.

A
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization
of a klt pair
(X,\Delta)
is a projective crepant birational morphism
\pi\colon(Y,\Delta_{Y})\rightarrow(X,\Delta)
such that
(Y,\Delta_{Y})
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminal pair. We also say that
(Y,\Delta_{Y})
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization
of
(X,\Delta)
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a klt pair and let
(Y,D)
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminal pair with a projective birational morphism
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow X
such that
K_{Y}+D=\pi^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta)+E
for some
\pi
-exceptional
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
E\geq 0
. Then any face-contracting
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,\Delta)=:(X_{0},\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1},\Delta_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{i},\Delta_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}

can be lifted to a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,D)=:(Y_{0},D_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{1},D_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{i},D_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,\Delta)=:(X_{0},\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1},\Delta_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{i},\Delta_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}=\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}

where each
\psi_{i}
is a sequence of
(K_{Y_{i}}+B_{i})
-MMP with
0\leq B_{i}\leq D_{i}
defined by

(2.5)

\mathrm{mult}_{F}B_{i}=\max\{0,-a(F,X_{i+1},\Delta_{i+1})\}

for all prime divisors
F
on
Y_{i}
, the map
\psi_{i}
does not contract any component of
B_{i}
, and
D_{i+1}=(\psi_{i})_{*}B_{i}
. Moreover,
\pi_{i}:(Y_{i},D_{i})\rightarrow(X_{i},\Delta_{i})
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization for every
i\geq 1
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
2.15
and proceeds by induction on
i
. Let
f_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow Z_{i}
and
f_{i}^{+}:X_{i+1}\rightarrow Z_{i}
be the associated birational fibrations of
\varphi_{i}
. When
i=0
, set
(Y_{0},D_{0}):=(Y,D)
and
\pi_{0}:=\pi
. For
i\geq 1
, suppose that
(Y_{i-1},D_{i-1})
has been constructed. Define
B_{i-1}
by (
2.5
). Clearly
B_{i-1}\geq 0
. By the negativity lemma and the induction hypothesis that
K_{Y_{i-1}}+D_{i-1}\geq\pi_{i-1}^{*}(K_{i-1}+\Delta_{i-1})
, we have

a(F,Y_{i-1},D_{i-1})\leq a(F,X_{i-1},\Delta_{i-1})\leq a(F,X_{i},\Delta_{i})

for all divisors
F
over
X
, hence
B_{i-1}\leq D_{i-1}
. By
[
42
]
*Lemma 3.38 and
[
8
]
*Lemma 3.6.6(4),
(X_{i},\Delta_{i})
is the ample model over
Z_{i-1}
of
(Y_{i-1},B_{i-1})
. Thus as in the proof of Lemma
2.15
, by
[
11
]
*Theorem 1.9 we may run a
(K_{Y_{i-1}}+B_{i-1})
-MMP
\psi_{i-1}:(Y_{i-1},B_{i-1})\dashrightarrow(Y_{i},D_{i})
over
Z_{i-1}
, where
D_{i}:=(\psi_{i-1})_{*}B_{i-1}
, such that
K_{Y_{i}}+D_{i}
is nef over
Z_{i-1}
and
(X_{i},\Delta_{i})
is the ample model over
Z_{i-1}
of
(Y_{i},D_{i})
, which gives the morphism
\pi_{i}
. If
F
is a component of
B_{i-1}
, then we have

\mathrm{mult}_{F}B_{i-1}=-a(F,X_{i},\Delta_{i})=-a(F,Y_{i},D_{i})\leq-a(F,Y_{i-1},B_{i-1})=\mathrm{mult}_{F}B_{i-1},

thus the inequality in the middle is an equality; in particular,
F
is not contracted by the MMP. By
[
42
]
*Corollary 3.43,
(Y_{i},D_{i})
is terminal. We finish the induction.
∎

We will frequently use the following observation to reduce questions about general type MMPs to MMPs with a big boundary.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a klt pair and
X\rightarrow T
a morphism.

(1)

If
K_{X}+\Delta
is big over
T
, then there exists a klt pair
(X,\Delta^{\prime})
, such that
K_{X}+\Delta^{\prime}\sim_{\mathbb{R},T}\mu(K_{X}+\Delta)
for some
\mu>0
and
\Delta^{\prime}
is big.

(2)

If
\Delta
is big over
T
, then there exists a klt pair
(X,\Delta_{0})
such that
K_{X}+\Delta\sim_{\mathbb{R},T}K_{X}+\Delta_{0}
and
\Delta_{0}\geq A\geq 0
for some
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
A
that is ample over
T
.

Proof.

(1) We have
K_{X}+\Delta\sim_{\mathbb{R},T}D
for some big
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D\geq 0
. Then
(X,\Delta^{\prime}:=\Delta+\varepsilon D)
is klt for some
\varepsilon>0
, and
K_{X}+\Delta+\varepsilon D\sim_{\mathbb{R},T}(1+\varepsilon)(K_{X}+\Delta)
.
We may let
\mu=1+\varepsilon
.

(2) Write
\Delta\sim_{\mathbb{R},T}H+E
for some ample
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
H\geq 0
and
E\geq 0
. We may take
\Delta_{0}:=(1-t)\Delta+t(H+E)
and
A:=tH
for some
0<t\ll 1
.
∎

The following corollary of the negativity lemma is needed in the proof of special termination in Section
5
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Consider a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{+}}

where
f,f^{+}
are birational fibrations. Let
S
be a prime divisor on
X
such that
\varphi
is an isomorphism at the generic point of
S
, and let
S^{+}
be the strict transform of
S
on
X^{+}
. Let
\Delta\geq 0
be an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
X
such that
(X,S+\Delta)
is dlt, and let
\Delta^{+}\geq 0
be an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
X^{+}
.
Assume that

(1)

f_{*}\Delta=f^{+}_{*}\Delta^{+}
,

(2)

-(K_{X}+S+\Delta)
is
f
-ample,

(3)

K_{X^{+}}+S^{+}+\Delta^{+}
is
f^{+}
-ample, and

(4)

the induced birational map
(S,\mathrm{Diff}_{S}(\Delta))\dashrightarrow(S^{+},\mathrm{Diff}_{S^{+}}(\Delta^{+}))
extends to an isomorphism of pairs.

Then
\varphi
extends to an isomorphism around
S
.

Proof.

Let
g\colon Y\rightarrow X
and
h\colon Y\rightarrow X^{+}
be a common resolution such that
h=\varphi\circ g
. Let
\psi\colon Y\to Z
be the induced morphism, i.e.
\psi:=f\circ g=f^{+}\circ h
. Write

g^{*}(K_{X}+S+\Delta)=h^{*}(K_{X^{+}}+S^{+}+\Delta^{+})+E

for some
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
E
. Let
S_{Y}:=g^{-1}_{*}S
, then we also have
S_{Y}=h^{-1}_{*}S^{+}
, hence
S_{Y}\not\subseteq\mathrm{Supp}(E)
. By adjunction, we get

g|_{S_{Y}}^{*}(K_{S}+\mathrm{Diff}_{S}(\Delta))=h|_{S_{Y}}^{*}(K_{S^{+}}+\mathrm{Diff}_{S^{+}}(\Delta^{+}))+E|_{S_{Y}}.

By assumption (4), we also have

g|_{S_{Y}}^{*}(K_{S}+\mathrm{Diff}_{S}(\Delta))=h|_{S_{Y}}^{*}(K_{S^{+}}+\mathrm{Diff}_{S^{+}}(\Delta^{+})).

Combined with the previous equality we see that
E|_{S_{Y}}=0
. By assumption (1), the
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
E
is exceptional over
Z
. By assumptions (2) and (3),
-E
is nef over
Z
, hence by
[
42
, Lemma 3.39]
, we have
E\geq 0
, and for any closed point
z\in Z
, either
\psi^{-1}(z)\cap\mathrm{Supp}(E)=\emptyset
or
\psi^{-1}(z)\subseteq\mathrm{Supp}(E)
. Since
E|_{S_{Y}}=0
, this implies that

\psi(\mathrm{Supp}(E))\cap\psi(S_{Y})=\emptyset.

Therefore, for any prime divisor
D
over
Z
with
\mathrm{Center}_{Z}(D)\cap\psi(S_{Y})\neq\emptyset
we have

(2.6)

a(D,X,S+\Delta)=a(D,X^{+},S^{+}+\Delta^{+}).

If
f
is not an isomorphism around
S
, then there exists a prime divisor
D
over
X
such that
\mathrm{Center}_{X}(D)\cap S\neq\emptyset
and
\mathrm{Center}_{X}(D)\subseteq\mathrm{Ex}(f)
. In particular,

\mathrm{Center}_{Z}(D)\cap\psi(S_{Y})=f(\mathrm{Center}_{X}(D))\cap f(S)\neq\emptyset.

The negativity lemma
[
42
, Lemma 3.38]
and assumptions (2) and (3) then gives

a(D,X,S+\Delta)<a(D,X^{+},S^{+}+\Delta^{+}),

which contradicts (
2.6
). We get a similar contradiction if
f^{+}
is not an isomorphism around
S^{+}
. Thus
f
and
f^{+}
are isomorphisms around
S
and
S^{+}
respectively, and hence
\varphi
is an isomorphism near
S
.
∎

We also need the termination of
4
-fold terminal MMP.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminal pair of dimension
\leq 4
. Then every
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,\Delta)=:(X_{0},\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1},\Delta_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{i},\Delta_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}

where
(X_{i},\Delta_{i})
is terminal for all
i
, must terminate after finitely many steps.

Proof.

The same proof of
[
21
,
22
]
in the projective setting for
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors works here verbatim.
∎

2.7.
\mathbb{T}
-singularities

In this subsection, we collect some results concerning singularities with a torus action. Such singularities naturally appear in the local K-stability theory of klt singularities. We only need the results in this subsection when we prove the boundedness of the fibers of extremal contractions and flips in Section
4
.

Let
X=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
be an affine normal variety and let
\rho\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathrm{Aut}(X)
be a one-parameter subgroup. Then it induces a weight decomposition
R=\oplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}R_{m}
and a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant valuation
\mathrm{wt}_{\rho}
on
X
such that
\mathrm{wt}_{\rho}(f)=m
when
0\neq f\in R_{m}
.

In general, let
\mathbb{T}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(X)
be an algebraic torus (i.e.
\mathbb{T}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}^{r}
for some
r>0
). Let
N:=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathbb{T})
be the co-weight lattice, and
M:=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{G}_{m})
the weight lattice. Then we have a weight decomposition

R=\oplus_{\alpha\in M}R_{\alpha}.

For any
f\in R
, we denote by
f_{\alpha}
the component of weight
\alpha\in M
in this weight decomposition. For any
0\neq\xi\in N_{\mathbb{R}}
, we can define a valuation
\mathrm{wt}_{\xi}
on
X
by

\mathrm{wt}_{\xi}(f):=\min\{\langle\xi,\alpha\rangle\mid\alpha\in M,f_{\alpha}\neq 0\}.

A one-parameter subgroup
\rho\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathbb{T}
can be viewed as a primitive element of
N
. The corresponding valuation
\mathrm{wt}_{\rho}
is exactly the one defined in the previous paragraph.

We say that the
\mathbb{T}
-action on
X
is
good
if there is a unique closed point
x\in X
that is in the orbit closure of any
\mathbb{T}
-orbit. This is equivalent to the condition that
R_{0}=\mathbb{C}
. The
weight monoid

S
consists of all
\alpha\in M
such that
R_{\alpha}\neq 0
, and the
weight cone

\sigma\subseteq M_{\mathbb{R}}
is defined as the (convex, closed, polyhedral) cone generated by
S
. Note that the valuation
\mathrm{wt}_{\xi}
has a center in
X
if and only if
\xi
is contained in the dual
\sigma^{\vee}
of the weight cone.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a log pair and
\mathbb{T}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(X,\Delta)
a torus. Assume that
X
is affine. Then there exists some
\alpha\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
A_{X,\Delta}(\mathrm{wt}_{\xi})=\langle\xi,\alpha\rangle
for all
\xi\in\sigma^{\vee}
, where
\sigma\subseteq M_{\mathbb{R}}
is the weight cone.

Proof.

(
cf.

[
50
]
*Theorem 2.15(3).) By
[
3
, Theorems 3.1 and 3.4]
, there exist a proper birational
\mathbb{T}
-equivariant morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{X}\to X
and a morphism
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\to Y=\widetilde{X}/\!\!/\mathbb{T}
whose generic fiber
F
is an affine toric variety. In particular,
X
is
\mathbb{T}
-equivariantly birational to
\mathbb{T}\times Y
(where
\mathbb{T}
acts trivially on
Y
). Let
K_{\widetilde{X}}+\widetilde{\Delta}=\varphi^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta)
be the crepant pullback. Then
\widetilde{\Delta}|_{F}
is a torus invariant
\mathbb{R}
-divisor. Since
A_{X,\Delta}(\mathrm{wt}_{\xi})=A_{\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\Delta}}(\mathrm{wt}_{\xi})
and the center of
\mathrm{wt}_{\xi}
dominates
Y
, it suffices to compute the log discrepancy along the generic fiber
F
of
\pi
. Thus we reduce to the toric case, where the desired statement is well known (see, for example,
[
4
]
*Section 4).
∎

Given a morphism
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
, we call a torus
\mathbb{T}
-action on
\mathcal{X}
that commutes with the trivial
\mathbb{T}
-action on
\mathcal{B}
a
fiberwise
\mathbb{T}
-action
. We next observe that the weight cone and the log discrepancy function are locally constant for any
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of klt singularities with a fiberwise torus action.

Definition 2.22
.

We call
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{B}
an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of pairs
if

(1)

f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
is an affine, flat morphism,

(2)

for any closed point
b\in\mathcal{B}
, the fiber
\mathcal{X}_{b}
is connected and normal,

(3)

\mathcal{D}\geq 0
is an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
\mathcal{X}
whose support does not contain any fiber
\mathcal{X}_{b}
of
f
,

(4)

\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{B}
are normal, and
K_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}+\mathcal{D}
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier.

If
f\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{B}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of pairs, and
B\subseteq\mathcal{X}
is a section of
f
, We call
\mathcal{B}\subseteq(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{B}
an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of singularities
.
If in addition
b\in(\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b})
is a klt singularity for any closed point
b\in\mathcal{B}
, we say it is an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of klt singularities
.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
be an affine, surjective morphism of varieties with a fiberwise torus
\mathbb{T}
-action. Then the weight monoid of the
\mathbb{T}
-action on the fiber
\mathcal{X}_{b}
is locally constant as
b\in\mathcal{B}
varies.

Proof.

We may assume that both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{X}
are affine, and
\mathcal{B}
is connected. Let
\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
. It suffices to show that for any
\alpha\in M
with
R_{\alpha}\neq 0
, we have
R_{\alpha}|_{\mathcal{X}_{b}}\neq 0
for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
. Since we have a fiberwise
\mathbb{T}
-action, the morphism
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
factors through
X_{0}:=\mathcal{X}/\!\!/\mathbb{T}=\mathrm{Spec}(R_{0})
, thus we may replace
\mathcal{B}
by
X_{0}
to show that the restriction
R_{\alpha}|_{\mathcal{X}_{b}}
is nonzero. Since
R
is generated by finitely many homogeneous elements, it is not hard to see that each
R_{\alpha}
is finitely generated over
R_{0}
. Alternatively, this also follows directly from
[
3
, Theorems 3.1 and 3.4]
: there exist a projective morphism
Y\to\mathrm{Spec}(R_{0})
and a semiample line bundle
L_{\alpha}
(one for each
\alpha
in the weight monoid of
R
) on
Y
such that
R_{\alpha}=H^{0}(Y,L_{\alpha})
. If
R_{\alpha}|_{X_{b}}=0
, then Nakayama’s lemma implies that
R_{\alpha}=0
in a neighborhood of
b\in X_{0}
, thus
R_{\alpha}
is a torsion
R_{0}
-module. Since
R
is an integral domain, we deduce that
R_{\alpha}=0
. This proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{B}
be an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of singularities with a fiberwise torus
\mathbb{T}
-action and let
\sigma
be the weight cone of the this
\mathbb{T}
-action. Then for any
\xi\in\sigma^{\vee}
, the log discrepancy function
b\mapsto A_{\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b}}(\mathrm{wt}_{\xi})
is locally constant on
\mathcal{B}
.

Note that the morphism
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
is surjective since it has a section. Thus by Lemma
2.23
, the weight monoid
S
and the weight cone
\sigma
of the
\mathbb{T}
-action on the fiber
b\in(\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b})
is locally constant in the family.

Proof.

We may assume that
\mathcal{B}
is affine and connected. Since the log discrepancy function is linear on the weight cone by Lemma
2.21
, it suffices to prove the statement when
\xi
is a primitive element in the dual
S^{\vee}
of the weight monoid. In this case
\xi
corresponds to a one-parameter subgroup of
\mathbb{T}
. After replacing
\mathbb{T}
by this one-parameter subgroup, we reduce to the case when
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{R})
. Then we have a weight decomposition
\mathcal{R}=\oplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{R}_{m}
. Let
\mathfrak{a}_{m}:=\sum_{k\geq m}\mathcal{R}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{Y}:=\mathrm{Proj}\left(\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{a}_{m}\right)
. Then
\mathfrak{a}_{m}|_{\mathcal{X}_{b}}
is the valuation ideal of
\mathrm{wt}_{\xi}
(as a valuation on
\mathcal{X}_{b}
), and we have a proper birational morphism
\pi\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
with a unique exceptional divisor
\mathcal{E}\cong\mathrm{Proj}(\mathcal{R})
. Note that
\mathcal{E}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, we have
\mathcal{E}_{b}\cong\mathrm{Proj}(\mathcal{R}_{b})
(where
\mathcal{R}_{b}:=\mathcal{R}\otimes k(b)
) is a prime divisor on
\mathcal{Y}_{b}
for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
(since
\mathcal{X}_{b}\cong\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{R}_{b})
is integral), and
\mathrm{wt}_{\xi}=\mathrm{ord}_{\mathcal{E}_{b}}
as valuations on
\mathcal{X}_{b}
. Let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}
be the strict transform of
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathcal{Y}
. Then

K_{\mathcal{Y}}+D_{\mathcal{Y}}+\mathcal{E}\sim_{\mathbb{R}}\pi^{*}(K_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathcal{D})+a\mathcal{E}

where
a=A_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{E})
. As the family
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{B}
is
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein, by restricting to the fibers we get

K_{\mathcal{Y}_{b}}+D_{\mathcal{Y},b}+\mathcal{E}_{b}\sim_{\mathbb{R}}\pi^{*}(K_{\mathcal{X}_{b}}+\mathcal{D}_{b})+a\mathcal{E}_{b}

for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
; in other words,
A_{\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b}}(\mathrm{wt}_{\xi})=A_{\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b}}(\mathcal{E}_{b})=a
is independent of
b\in\mathcal{B}
.
∎

We also need the special boundedness of klt singularities when the local volume is bounded away from zero. To state the precise result, we need some definitions.

Definition 2.25
.

A
test configuration
of a singularity
x\in(X,\Delta)
consists of a test configuration
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
of the pair
(X,\Delta)
and a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant section
\sigma\colon\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathcal{X}
such that
\sigma(t)=x
for
t\neq 0
under the isomorphism
(\mathcal{X}_{t},\mathcal{D}_{t})\cong(X,\Delta)
. We still denote the test configuration by
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
as the section
\sigma
is uniquely determined by this data. When
(X,\Delta)
is klt, the test configuration
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is called
special
if
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{0}+\mathcal{D})
is plt. In this case
(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{0})
is also klt (by adjunction) and we call
\sigma(0)\in(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{0})
a
special degeneration
of
x\in(X,\Delta)
.

Example 2.26
.

Let
x\in(X=\mathrm{Spec}(R),\Delta)
be a singularity and let
E
be a prime divisor over
x\in X
. Let

\mathfrak{a}_{m}:=\{f\in R\,|\,\mathrm{ord}_{E}(f)\geq m\}.

Assume that the graded algebra
\mathrm{gr}_{E}R:=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{a}_{m}/\mathfrak{a}_{m+1}
is finitely generated. Then so is
\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{a}_{m}
. By the Rees construction, we get a flat family
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{R})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
where

\mathcal{R}=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}t^{-m}\mathfrak{a}_{m}

is the (extended) Rees algebra and
t
is the affine coordinate on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. The
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on
\mathcal{R}
induces a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on the family and this gives a test configuration of the singularity
x\in X
. Note that
\mathcal{X}_{0}\cong\mathrm{Spec}(\mathrm{gr}_{E}R)
, and
\mathrm{gr}_{E}R
is an integral domain, hence
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is integral.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be the closure of
\Delta\times(\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
in
\mathcal{X}
under the isomorphism
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{0}\cong X\times(\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
. Then
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is a test configuration of
x\in(X,\Delta)
. Let us prove that if
K_{X}+\Delta
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier, then so is
K_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathcal{D}
. Indeed, by shrinking
X
around
x
(which preserves
\mathrm{gr}_{E}R
and thus does not change the central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
) we may assume that
K_{X}+\Delta\sim_{\mathbb{R}}0
, thus
K_{\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{0}}+\mathcal{D}|_{\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{0}}\sim_{\mathbb{R}}0
. As
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is irreducible, we deduce that
K_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathcal{D}\sim_{\mathbb{R}}c\mathcal{X}_{0}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. But
\mathcal{X}_{0}\sim_{\mathbb{R}}0
, hence
K_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathcal{D}\sim_{\mathbb{R}}0
. In particular,
K_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathcal{D}
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier. It follows (by inversion of adjunction) that if
x\in(X,\Delta)
is klt, then
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is a special test configuration if and only if
(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{0})
is klt.

The following special boundedness statement is one of the main results in the local K-stability theory of klt singularities.

Theorem 2.27
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon>0
and let
I\subseteq[0,1]
be a finite set. Then there exists an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family
\mathcal{B}\subseteq(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{B}
of klt singularities with the action of an algebraic torus
\mathbb{T}
over
\mathcal{B}
, such that:

For any klt singularity
x\in(X,\Delta)
of dimension
n
, local volume at least
\varepsilon
and coefficients in
I
, and any torus
\mathbb{T}_{0}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(X,\Delta)
, there exists a
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-equivariant special degeneration of
x\in(X,\Delta)
to
b\in(\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b})
for some
b\in\mathcal{B}
that identifies
\mathbb{T}_{0}
with a subtorus of
\mathbb{T}_{b}
.

Proof.

This essentially follows from the stable degeneration theory of klt singularities together with the main results of
[
65
]
. We refer to
[
65
, Section 2.2-2.3]
for the relevant definitions that appear in this proof. Enlarging the finite set
I
if necessarily, we may assume that for any
a,b\in I
with
a+b\leq 1
we have
a+b\in I
. By the Stable Degeneration Theorem
[
13
,
50
,
67
,
64
,
66
]
(see also
[
69
]
for a survey and
[
68
]
for the extension to real coefficients), for every klt singularity
x\in(X=\mathrm{Spec}(R),\Delta)
, up to rescaling there is a unique and hence
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-invariant quasi-monomial valuation
v
minimizing the normalized volume function
\widehat{\rm vol}_{X,\Delta}
. It induces a
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-equivariant degeneration of
x\in(X,\Delta)
to a K-semistable log Fano cone singularity
x_{0}\in(X_{0}=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathrm{gr}_{v}R),\Delta_{0};\xi_{v})
with

\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)=\widehat{\rm vol}(x_{0},X_{0},\Delta_{0}).

Note that this implies that the torus
\langle\xi_{v}\rangle
generated by the Reeb vector field
\xi_{v}
commutes with
\mathbb{T}_{0}
. Here
\mathrm{gr}_{v}R
is defined by
\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{a}_{\lambda}/\mathfrak{a}_{>\lambda}
where
\mathfrak{a}_{\lambda}=\{f\in R\,|\,v(f)\geq\lambda\}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{>\lambda}=\{f\in R\,|\,v(f)>\lambda\}
.

We claim that
x_{0}\in(X_{0},\Delta_{0})
can be realized as a
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-equivariant special degeneration of
x\in(X,\Delta)
. Indeed, by
[
50
, Lemma 2.10]
, there exists some divisorial valuation
v_{0}
over
x\in X
(obtained from a small perturbation of the weights defining the quasi-monomial valuations
v
) such that
\mathrm{gr}_{v}R\cong\mathrm{gr}_{v_{0}}R
. Moreover, since
v
is
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-invariant, the construction in the proof of
loc. cit.
can be made
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-equivariant (for example, we can choose
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-invariant generators of
\mathrm{gr}_{v}R
as well as their lifts to
R
), thus we may assume that the perturbed valuation
v_{0}
is also
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-invariant. Write
v_{0}=\lambda\cdot\mathrm{ord}_{E}
where
E
is a prime divisor over
x\in X
. Since
\mathrm{gr}_{E}R\cong\mathrm{gr}_{v_{0}}R\cong\mathrm{gr}_{v}R
, the divisor
E
induces a
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-equivariant special degeneration of
x\in(X,\Delta)
to
x_{0}\in(X_{0},\Delta_{0})
by the construction in Example
2.26
. This proves the claim.

By construction,
\widehat{\rm vol}(x_{0},X_{0},\Delta_{0})\geq\varepsilon
and the coefficients of
\Delta_{0}
belong to
I
. Since
x_{0}\in(X_{0},\Delta_{0};\xi_{v})
is K-semistable, we also have
\Theta(X_{0},\Delta_{0};\xi_{v})=1
where
\Theta(X_{0},\Delta_{0};\xi_{v})
denotes the volume ratio (see
[
65
, Definition 2.7]
). By perturbing
\xi_{v}
in the Reeb cone, we can choose some Reeb vector field
\xi_{0}
such that the torus
\langle\xi_{0}\rangle
it generates contains both
\langle\xi_{v}\rangle
and
\mathbb{T}_{0}
and we have
\Theta(X_{0},\Delta_{0};\xi_{0})\geq\frac{1}{2}
(this uses the continuity of the normalized volume function on the Reeb cone, see
[
68
, Lemma 2.11]
). By
[
65
, Theorem 1.4]
, such polarized log Fano cone singularities
x_{0}\in(X_{0},\Delta_{0};\xi_{0})
are bounded. By definition (see
[
65
, Definition 2.16]
), this means that there exists an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family
\mathcal{B}\subseteq(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{B}
of klt singularities with the action of an algebraic torus
\mathbb{T}
over
\mathcal{B}
, such that
x_{0}\in(X_{0},\Delta_{0};\xi_{0})
is isomorphic to some fiber
b\in(\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b})
and
\xi_{0}
is contained in the Reeb cone of
\mathbb{T}_{b}
. Thus
\mathbb{T}_{b}
contains the torus generated by
\xi_{0}
and in particular it contains
\mathbb{T}_{0}
. This proves the theorem.
∎

2.8.
Log Fano and log Calabi-Yau fibrations

A key step in bounding the fibers of extremal contractions and flips (Section
4
) is the special boundedness of certain log Fano and log Calabi-Yau fibrations. In this subsection, we discuss some properties of such fibrations and define their special boundedness.

Definition 2.28
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a pair and
X\to Z
a projective morphism.

(1)

We say that
(X,\Delta)
is
log Fano
over
Z
if
(X,\Delta)
is klt and
-(K_{X}+\Delta)
is ample over
Z
.

(2)

We say that
(X,\Delta)
is
log Calabi-Yau
over
Z
if
(X,\Delta)
is log canonical and
K_{X}+\Delta\sim_{\mathbb{R},Z}0
.

(3)

We say that
(X,\Delta)
is
of Fano type
over
Z
if there exists some
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
\Delta^{\prime}\geq\Delta
such that
(X,\Delta^{\prime})
is log Fano over
Z
. Equivalently,
-(K_{X}+\Delta)
is big over
Z
and there exists some
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
0\leq D\sim_{\mathbb{R},Z}-(K_{X}+\Delta)
such that
(X,\Delta+D)
is klt.

A fibration
(X,\Delta)\to Z
(or fibration germ
(X,\Delta)\to Z\ni z
) is called a
log Fano
(resp.
log Calabi-Yau
, resp.
Fano type
)
fibration
(or
fibration germ
) if
(X,\Delta)
is log Fano (resp. log Calabi-Yau, resp. of Fano type) over
Z
.

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
f\colon(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})\dashrightarrow(X,\Delta)
be a birational map between pairs that are projective over
Z
. Assume that
K_{X}+\Delta
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier,
f^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta)\geq K_{X^{\prime}}+\Delta^{\prime}
, and
(X,\Delta)
is of Fano type over
Z
. Then
(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
is also of Fano type over
Z
.

Proof.

Since
(X,\Delta)
is of Fano type over
Z
, we know that
-(K_{X}+\Delta)
is big over
Z
and there exists some
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
0\leq D\sim_{\mathbb{R},Z}-(K_{X}+\Delta)
such that
(X,\Delta+D)
is klt. As
f^{*}(K_{X}+\Delta)\geq K_{X^{\prime}}+\Delta^{\prime}
, by taking crepant pullback we see that both conditions are also satisfied by
(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
, hence
(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
is of Fano type over
Z
.
∎

Definition 2.30
.

A
test configuration
of a fibration
f\colon(X,\Delta)\to Z
consists of a test configuration
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
of the pair
(X,\Delta)
and a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant fibration
\widetilde{f}\colon(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, where
\mathcal{Z}
is a test configuration of
Z
, such that
\widetilde{f}|_{\mathcal{X}_{t}}=f
for all
0\neq t\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
. For simplicity, we denote the test configuration by
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
. Test configuration of a fibration germ
(X,\Delta)\to Z\ni z
is defined as a test configuration
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
of the fibration
(X,\Delta)\to Z
such that
\mathcal{Z}
is also a test configuration of the singularity
z\in Z
.

A test configuration
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
of a log Fano fibration
(X,\Delta)\to Z
is called
special
if
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{0}+\mathcal{D})
is plt,
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is normal and
-(K_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathcal{D})
is ample over
\mathcal{Z}
. Note that under these conditions
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is normal by
[
47
]
*Theorem 4.16. In this case
(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{0})\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is also a log Fano fibration and we call it a
special degeneration
of
(X,\Delta)\to Z
. Special test configurations and special degenerations of a log Fano fibration germ are defined analogously.

Definition 2.31
.

We say that
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of log Fano fibrations
over a finite type base
\mathcal{B}
if:

(1)

Both
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}
are flat over
\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Z}
is a fibration,

(2)

for any
b\in\mathcal{B}
, the fibers
\mathcal{X}_{b}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{b}
are connected and normal,

(3)

\mathcal{D}\geq 0
is an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
\mathcal{X}
whose support does not contain any fiber
\mathcal{X}_{b}
of
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
,

(4)

\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{B}
are normal,
-(K_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}+\mathcal{D})
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier and ample over
\mathcal{Z}
, and

(5)

the pair
(\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b})
is klt for any closed point
b\in\mathcal{B}
.

In particular,
(\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b})\to\mathcal{Z}_{b}
is a log Fano fibration for every
b\in\mathcal{B}
.

Definition 2.32
.

We say that a set
\mathcal{S}
of Fano type fibrations is
bounded
if there exist an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of log Fano fibration
(\mathcal{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})\to\mathcal{Z}
over a finite type base
\mathcal{B}
and an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
0\leq\mathcal{D}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, such that any Fano type fibration
(X,\Delta)\to Z
in
\mathcal{S}
is isomorphic to
(\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b})\to\mathcal{Z}_{b}
for some
b\in\mathcal{B}
.

For log Fano fibrations, it is tempting to define their boundedness by requiring
\mathcal{D}=\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
in the above definition. The next lemma shows that these two definitions are equivalent.

Lemma 2.33
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of log Fano fibrations. Assume that
\mathcal{S}
is bounded as a set of Fano type fibrations. Then there exists a
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
of log Fano fibrations over a finite type base
\mathcal{B}
such that any log Fano fibration
(X,\Delta)\to Z
in
\mathcal{S}
is isomorphic to
(\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathcal{D}_{b})\to\mathcal{Z}_{b}
for some
b\in\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})\to\mathcal{Z}
and
0\leq\mathcal{D}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
be as in Definition
2.32
. Then there exists some constant
\varepsilon>0
such that the all fibers of
(\mathcal{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})\to\mathcal{B}
are
\varepsilon
-klt (i.e. their total minimal log discrepancies are larger than
\varepsilon
). It follows that for any
(X,\Delta)\to Z
in
\mathcal{S}
we also have
(X,\Delta)
is
\varepsilon
-klt. By
[
27
, Proposition 2.4]
and Noetherian induction, we deduce that possibly after stratifying
\mathcal{B}
, we may assume that
K_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}}+\mathcal{D}
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier. Thus
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of log Fano fibrations and we are done.
∎

Definition 2.34
.

Let
(X,\Delta)\to Z
be a Fano type fibration (or fibration germ). A test configuration
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
of
(X,\Delta)\to Z
is said to be
of Fano type
if there exists an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}\geq\mathcal{D}\geq 0
on
\mathcal{X}
such that
(\mathcal{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})\to\mathcal{Z}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-Gorenstein family of log Fano fibrations over
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. In particular,
(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{0})\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is a Fano type fibration. In this case we also say that
(X,\Delta)\to Z
degenerates to
(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{0})\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}
by a Fano type test configuration.

Lemma 2.35
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
be a Fano type test configuration (of some fibration), let
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{Z}
be another test configuration (of a possibly different fibration), and let
f\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}
be a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant birational map over
\mathcal{Z}
. Assume that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
is irreducible,
f
is an isomorphism at the generic point of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and
f^{*}(K_{\mathcal{X}}+{\mathcal{D}})\geq K_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}+\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Then
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{Z}
is also a Fano type test configuration.

Proof.

The condition that
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{Z}
is a Fano type test configuration is equivalent to saying that
-(K_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathcal{D})
is big over
\mathcal{Z}
and there exists some
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
0\leq\mathcal{G}\sim_{\mathbb{R},\mathcal{Z}}-(K_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathcal{D})
on
\mathcal{X}
such that
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{t}+\mathcal{D}+\mathcal{G})
is plt for every
t\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
. These conditions are preserved by the given birational map
f
.
∎

Definition 2.36
.

We say that a set
\mathcal{S}
of log Fano fibrations germs is
specially bounded
if there exists a bounded set
\mathcal{T}
of log Fano fibrations such that any log Fano fibration germ
(X,\Delta)\to Z\ni z
in
\mathcal{S}
specially degenerates to some
(X_{0},\Delta_{0})\to Z_{0}\ni z_{0}
in
\mathcal{T}
.

We say that a set
\mathcal{S}
of Fano type fibrations germs is
bounded up to Fano type degenerations
if there exists a bounded set
\mathcal{T}
of Fano type fibrations such that any Fano type fibration germ
(X,\Delta)\to Z\ni z
in
\mathcal{S}
degenerates to some
(X_{0},\Delta_{0})\to Z_{0}\ni z_{0}
in
\mathcal{T}
by a Fano type test configuration.

Finally, we say that a set
\mathcal{S}
of fibration germs is
log specially bounded
if there exists a bounded set
\mathcal{T}
of Fano type fibrations, such that for any fibration germ
(X,\Delta)\to Z\ni z
in
\mathcal{S}
, we can find an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D\geq 0
on
X
with
\mathrm{Supp}(\Delta)\subseteq\mathrm{Supp}(D)
, a Fano type fibration
(X_{0},\Delta_{0})\to Z_{0}\ni z_{0}
in
\mathcal{T}
, and some
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D_{0}\geq 0
on
X_{0}
with
\mathrm{Supp}(D_{0})\subseteq\mathrm{Supp}(\Delta_{0})
, such that
(X,D)\to Z\ni z
is a Fano type fibration, and it degenerates to
(X_{0},D_{0})\to Z_{0}\ni z_{0}
by a Fano type test configuration. Intuitively, the conditions mean that
\mathrm{Supp}(\Delta)
degenerates into
\mathrm{Supp}(\Delta_{0})
by some Fano type test configuration.

3.
Boundedness of invariants in MMP

The major goal of this section is to prove the following theorem, which is a relative version of Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial klt pair and
X\rightarrow T
a projective morphism. Assume that either
K_{X}+\Delta
or
\Delta
is big over
T
. Then there exist a positive integer
r
and a finite set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
, depending only on
(X,\Delta)
and
\pi
, such that for any sequence of steps of a
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP
(X,\Delta)\dashrightarrow(Y,\Delta_{Y})
over
T
,

(1)

the Cartier index of any Weil divisor on
Y
is at most
r
, and

(2)

for any (not necessarily closed) point
y\in Y
, we have
\mathrm{mld}(y,Y,\Delta_{Y})\in S
.

3.1.
Local volumes in MMP type contractions

We shall deduce Theorem
3.1
from the following more general result on the boundedness of invariants in MMP type contractions (Definition
2.12
).

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
(X,\Delta+D)
be a klt pair where
\Delta,D\geq 0
and
D
is big. Then there exist a positive integer
r
and two real number
\varepsilon,A>0
, depending only on
X
,
\Delta
and
D
, such that for any MMP type contraction
\varphi\colon(X,\Delta+D)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}+D^{\prime})
with
\Delta^{\prime}\geq 0
and
D^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}D
, we have:

(1)

The Cartier index of any
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier Weil divisor on
X^{\prime}
is at most
r
,

(2)

for any (not necessarily closed) point
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
, we have
\mathrm{mld}(x^{\prime},X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}+D^{\prime})\leq A
, and

(3)

if
K_{X^{\prime}}+\Delta^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier, then
\widehat{\rm vol}(x^{\prime},X^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})\geq\varepsilon
for all closed points
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
.

Before we prove this more general result, let us explain how it implies Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
assuming Theorem
3.2
.

By Lemma
2.18
, there exists some
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
\Delta_{0}\geq 0
on
X
such that
(X,\Delta_{0})
is klt,
\Delta_{0}
is big, and any
(K_{X}+\Delta)
-MMP over
T
is also a
(K_{X}+\Delta_{0})
-MMP. Part (1) then follows directly from Theorem
3.2
(1). By Lemma
2.2
, this implies that the log discrepancies
A_{Y,\Delta_{Y}}(E)
of prime divisors
E
over
Y
belong to a discrete set
S_{0}
that depends only on the coefficients of
\Delta
and the index bound
r
. Note that
Y
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial. Thus by Theorem
3.2
(3), we also know that there exists some positive real number
\varepsilon
(depending only on
X
and
\Delta_{0}
) such that
\widehat{\rm vol}(y,Y)\geq\varepsilon
for any closed point
y\in Y
. Combined with Theorem
2.5
, we see that there exists some constant
A>0
(depending only on
\dim X
and
\varepsilon
) such that
\mathrm{mld}(y,Y)\leq A
for all points
y\in Y
, hence
\mathrm{mld}(y,Y,\Delta_{Y})\leq A
as well. It follows that
\mathrm{mld}(y,Y,\Delta_{Y})
belongs to the finite set
S:=S_{0}\cap[0,A]
.
∎

In the rest of this subsection, we prove Theorem
3.2
. Since local volume controls most other invariants such as index and mld, we will primarily focus on bounding the local volume away from zero. A key ingredient is the following generalization of
[
65
]
*Lemma 2.13 (
cf.

[
12
]
*Theorem D).

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a klt pair of dimension
n
, and let
V_{\bullet}
be an eventually birational graded linear series (see Section
2.5
) of some
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
L
on
X
. Then for any closed point
x\in X
we have

\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)\geq\alpha(X,\Delta;V_{\bullet})^{n}\cdot\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet}).

The proof is in turn based on the following auxiliary lemma that we will apply again in Section
4
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a klt pair of dimension
n
, let
x\in X
be a closed point, and let
c_{0}>c_{1}
be positive constants. Then for any sufficiently large integer
m
and any linear subspace
V\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
of dimension at least
c_{0}\frac{m^{n}}{n!}
, there exists some nonzero element
s\in V
such that

m\cdot\mathrm{lct}_{x}(X,\Delta;\{s=0\})\leq\left(\frac{\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)}{c_{1}}\right)^{1/n}.

Proof.

This is proved by the same argument as in
[
12
]
*Theorem D or
[
65
]
*Lemma 2.13. Let
c:=\left(\frac{\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)}{c_{1}}\right)^{-1/n}
and let
v
be a valuation over
x\in X
such that
\widehat{\rm vol}_{X,\Delta}(v)=\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)
. Since the normalized volume is rescaling invariant (i.e.
\widehat{\rm vol}_{X,\Delta}(\lambda v)=\widehat{\rm vol}_{X,\Delta}(v)
for all
v\in\mathrm{Val}_{X,x}
and
\lambda>0
), we may rescale the minimizing valuation
v
and assume that
A_{X,\Delta}(v)=1
. It follows that
\mathrm{vol}(v)=\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)
. Let
\mathfrak{a}_{\lambda}:=\mathfrak{a}_{\lambda}(v)
(
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
) be the valuation ideals of
v
. Since
c_{0}>c_{1}>0
, for sufficiently large
m
we have

\dim(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak{a}_{cm})=\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)\cdot\frac{(cm)^{n}}{n!}+o(m^{n})=\frac{c_{1}m^{n}}{n!}+o(m^{n})<\frac{c_{0}m^{n}}{n!}.

Thus for any linear subspace
V\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
of dimension at least
c_{0}\frac{m^{n}}{n!}
, the natural map
V\to\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak{a}_{cm}
cannot be injective. It follows that there exists some nonzero element
s\in V\cap\mathfrak{a}_{cm}(v)
; in particular
v(s)\geq cm
and

m\cdot\mathrm{lct}_{x}(X,\Delta;\{s=0\})\leq m\cdot\frac{A_{X,\Delta}(v)}{v(s)}\leq\frac{1}{c}=\left(\frac{\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)}{c_{1}}\right)^{1/n}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

First assume that
L
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. For each sufficiently divisible positive integer
m
, after choosing a local trivialization of
mL
around
x\in X
, we may view each
V_{m}
as a subspace of
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
. Let
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
and set
c_{0}=\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet})-\varepsilon
and
c_{1}=\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet})-2\varepsilon
. Then
\dim V_{m}\geq c_{0}\frac{m^{n}}{n!}
when
m\gg 0
. Thus Lemma
3.4
gives
\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)\geq c_{1}\cdot\alpha(X,\Delta;V_{\bullet})^{n}
. Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
and hence
c_{1}\to\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet})
, we get the desired result.

In general, choose some Cartier divisor
D\geq 0
on
X
such that
\mathrm{Supp}(L)\subseteq\mathrm{Supp}(D)
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists some
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
L^{\prime}
such that
L\leq L^{\prime}\leq L+\varepsilon D
. Let
V^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be the image of
V_{\bullet}
under the natural embedding
H^{0}(mL)\hookrightarrow H^{0}(mL^{\prime})
. Since
L^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, the previous case implies that

\widehat{\rm vol}(x,X,\Delta)\geq\alpha(X,\Delta;V^{\prime}_{\bullet})^{n}\cdot\mathrm{vol}(V^{\prime}_{\bullet})=\alpha(X,\Delta;V^{\prime}_{\bullet})^{n}\cdot\mathrm{vol}(V_{\bullet}).

On the other hand, as
|V^{\prime}_{m}|=|V_{m}|+m(L^{\prime}-L)
and
0\leq L^{\prime}-L\leq\varepsilon D
, by Lemma
2.6
, we deduce that

\left(\frac{1}{\alpha(X,\Delta;V_{\bullet})}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathrm{lct}(X,\Delta;D)}\right)^{-1}\leq\alpha(X,\Delta;V^{\prime}_{\bullet})\leq\alpha(X,\Delta;V_{\bullet}),

hence
\alpha(X,\Delta;V^{\prime}_{\bullet})
can be arbitrarily close to
\alpha(X,\Delta;V_{\bullet})
when
\varepsilon\to 0
. The proposition thus follows.
∎

In the context of Theorem
3.2
, the linear systems we shall use come from the following construction.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
(X,\Delta+D)
be a klt pair such that
